### Tool result

_source line 1269 · 2026-05-22T00:07:33.753549+00:00_

1	Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with seven facets across four models and four benchmarks, totaling 1.27 million evaluation records. The variance structure is format-conditional: on multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 42--49\% of total variance, while on the free-form benchmark GSM8K, model and prompt template each contribute 12--15\% with no single dominant source. Model$\times$item interaction (33.6\%) is the hidden second-largest component, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\% interaction). Multi-facet designs require 10.0$\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to reach a generalizability coefficient of 0.80 (55 versus 552 items). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and Decision study (D-study) budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
  "old_string": "crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across four models and four benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, and GSM8K), totaling 1.27 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 42--49\\% of total variance (MMLU 42\\%, ARC 49\\%, HellaSwag 45\\%), with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source at 32--38\\%. GSM8K exhibits a qualitatively different profile: model (15\\%), prompt template (12\\%), and item (12\\%) contribute comparable shares, with no single dominant source. This is a dichotomy, not a gradient. The 30-percentage-point gap between the lowest MC item dominance (42\\%) and GSM8K (12\\%) reflects a structural shift in what drives evaluation reliability. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection dominate. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult.",
  "new_string": "crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across eight models and five benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, GSM8K, and MATH), totaling 2.3 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 34--38\\% of total variance, with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source. Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) exhibit a different profile: model, prompt template, and item each contribute 12--24\\%, with no single dominant source. The gap between MC item dominance (34--38\\%) and free-form item shares (17--24\\%) reflects a systematic format-dependent pattern in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection matter as much as item selection. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (up to 33\\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult."
}
```

In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection matter as much as item selection. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (up to 33\\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult."
  }
}
```

</details>

  "old_string": "The preceding analyses use only MMLU, a multiple-choice benchmark. We now test whether the item-dominated variance structure generalizes across evaluation formats by extending to four benchmarks spanning two response formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Design.} The expanded design (exp-002) crosses 4 models $\\times$ 4 benchmarks $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 921,600 records. The benchmarks include three multiple-choice tasks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag) and one free-form generation task (GSM8K). The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\\sigma^2_{\\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance.\n\n% [PLACEHOLDER: expand to 8 models × 5 benchmarks (+ MATH) when data ready]\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Table~\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a qualitative structural shift between formats. For all three multiple-choice benchmarks, item difficulty is the dominant component (MMLU 42\\%, ARC 49\\%, HellaSwag 45\\%), and model$\\times$item interaction is the second largest (32--38\\%). This structure is consistent with the cross-model analysis in Section~4.2.\n\nGSM8K exhibits a fundamentally different variance profile. Item difficulty accounts for only 12\\% of variance. Instead, model (15\\%), prompt template (12\\%), and model$\\times$prompt interaction (9\\%) become the leading components. The residual term absorbs 35\\% of variance, indicating substantial unexplained variation from seed and temperature effects in free-form generation.",
  "new_string": "The preceding analyses use only MMLU, a multiple-choice benchmark. We now test whether the item-dominated variance structure generalizes across evaluation formats by extending to five benchmarks spanning two response formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Design.} The expanded design (exp-002) crosses 8 models $\\times$ 5 benchmarks $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 2,304,000 records. The benchmarks include three multiple-choice tasks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag) and two free-form generation tasks (GSM8K, MATH). The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\\sigma^2_{\\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Table~\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a systematic format-dependent pattern. For all three multiple-choice benchmarks, item difficulty is the dominant component (MMLU 38\\%, ARC 38\\%, HellaSwag 34\\%), and model$\\times$item interaction is the second largest. This structure is consistent with the cross-model analysis in Section~4.2.\n\nFree-form benchmarks exhibit a different variance profile. On GSM8K, item difficulty accounts for only 17\\% of variance; model, prompt template, and their interaction become the leading components. MATH shows an intermediate pattern with item difficulty at 24\\%, higher than GSM8K but well below the MC benchmarks."
}

We now test whether the item-dominated variance structure generalizes across evaluation formats by extending to five benchmarks spanning two response formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Design.} The expanded design (exp-002) crosses 8 models $\\times$ 5 benchmarks $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 2,304,000 records. The benchmarks include three multiple-choice tasks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag) and two free-form generation tasks (GSM8K, MATH). The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\\sigma^2_{\\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Table~\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a systematic format-dependent pattern. For all three multiple-choice benchmarks, item difficulty is the dominant component (MMLU 38\\%, ARC 38\\%, HellaSwag 34\\%), and model$\\times$item interaction is the second largest. This structure is consistent with the cross-model analysis in Section~4.2.\n\nFree-form benchmarks exhibit a different variance profile. On GSM8K, item difficulty accounts for only 17\\% of variance; model, prompt template, and their interaction become the leading components. MATH shows an intermediate pattern with item difficulty at 24\\%, higher than GSM8K but well below the MC benchmarks."
  }
}
```

  "old_string": "This dichotomy is not a continuous gradient. The gap between the lowest MC item dominance (MMLU, 42\\%) and GSM8K (12\\%) represents a qualitative shift in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection dominate. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes this structural contrast.",
  "new_string": "The variance structure is format-conditional: MC benchmarks concentrate variance in items (34--38\\%), while FF benchmarks distribute it more evenly across model, prompt, and item facets (12--24\\% each). This structural difference, however, does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag requires 171 items for $G \\geq 0.80$, the highest of all benchmarks, demonstrating that format alone does not predict evaluation cost. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the structural contrast.\n\nTo probe the mechanism behind item variance, we exploit the natural heterogeneity within MMLU's 57 subjects. Subjects with higher item difficulty dispersion (measured by $\\text{std}(p_i)$ across models) exhibit higher item variance shares, with Spearman $\\rho = 0.806$ ($p = 0.005$, $n = 10$ subjects). Notably, \\texttt{high\\_school\\_mathematics}, the only MC subject with open-ended numerical items, shows item variance of just 10.4\\%---below the free-form benchmark GSM8K (16.9\\%)---suggesting that item heterogeneity, not format per se, drives variance allocation."
}
```

  "old_string": "Treating four purposively selected models as a quasi-random facet requires empirical validation. We provide three forms of evidence.\n\nD-study cross-validation: variance components estimated from all $\\binom{4}{3} = 4$ three-model subsets predict the four-model generalizability coefficient. The predicted $G = 0.816$ for $n_\\text{model} = 4$ closely matches the observed $G = 0.820$ ($\\Delta = 0.004$). This agreement confirms that the model facet behaves as expected under random-effects assumptions.\n\nLeave-one-model-out (LOMO) stability: all four three-model subsets preserve \\texttt{item\\_id} as the dominant component and rank \\texttt{model$\\times$item} as the second-largest ($G_\\text{item}$ range: 0.747--0.793). The consistency of rankings across all subsets indicates that no single model disproportionately drives the observed variance structure.\n\nForward projection: D-study estimates predict $G = 0.893$ at eight models. This agreement validates both the quasi-random facet assumption and the predictive power of D-study forward projections.\n\nLeave-one-model-out (LOMO) stability: all eight seven-model subsets preserve \\texttt{item\\_id} as the dominant component and rank \\texttt{model$\\times$item} as the second-largest. _source line 1321 · 2026-05-22T00:12:53.501170+00:00_

1	\section{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Our findings are bounded by three scope constraints. First, the model sample comprises four open-weight 7--9B parameter models from four architecture families; the results do not generalize to API-based models, larger models, or fine-tuned variants. Second, all experiments run on single-GPU inference (NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000); multi-GPU tensor parallelism introduces additional numerical non-associativity that our design does not capture. Third, the benchmark sample covers three multiple-choice and one free-form task; other evaluation formats such as code generation, long-form text production, and multi-turn dialogue may exhibit different variance structures.

### Tool result

_source line 1331 · 2026-05-22T00:13:26.128453+00:00_

7	
8	We address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across eight models and five benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, GSM8K, and MATH), totaling 2.3 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 34--38\% of total variance, with model$\times$item interaction as the second-largest source. Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) exhibit a different profile: model, prompt template, and item each contribute 12--24\%, with no single dominant source. The gap between MC item dominance (34--38\%) and free-form item shares (17--24\%) reflects a systematic format-dependent pattern in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection matter as much as item selection. A second finding is that model$\times$item interaction (up to 33\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all four benchmarks.
9

_source line 1335 · 2026-05-22T00:13:38.578657+00:00_

5	
6	Existing studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decomposed variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\%, but the study included no cross-benchmark comparison and excluded infrastructure facets such as numerical precision. Studies of answer ordering \citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, but this work predates the LLM evaluation context where benchmark format introduces an additional structural variable. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.

80	
81	The variance structure is format-conditional: MC benchmarks concentrate variance in items (34--38\%), while FF benchmarks distribute it more evenly across model, prompt, and item facets (12--24\% each). This structural difference, however, does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag requires 171 items for $G \geq 0.80$, the highest of all benchmarks, demonstrating that format alone does not predict evaluation cost. Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the structural contrast.

20	
21	\paragraph{Results.} Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} presents the variance decomposition. Introducing model as a facet reshapes the structure: item difficulty drops from 58.4\% to 37.9\%, while model$\times$item interaction emerges as the second-largest component at up to 33\%. The model main effect remains below 1\%, confirming that models differ not in overall ability level but in \emph{which items} they find difficult.
22	
23	\begin{table}[t]
24	\centering
25	\caption{Variance decomposition for the cross-model MMLU design (8 models, BF16, 460,800 records). Components estimated via Henderson Method~I with 1,000-resample bootstrap CIs. $G_\text{item} = 0.896$.}
26	\label{tab:cross_model}
27	\small
28	\begin{tabular}{lrl}
29	\toprule
30	Component & Var.\% & 95\% CI \\
31	\midrule
32	\textbf{item\_id} & \textbf{37.9} & \\
33	\underline{model$\times$item} & \underline{33.0} & \\
34	residual & 23.1 & \\
35	prompt$\times$item & 2.1 & \\
36	model & 0.9 & \\
37	seed$\times$item & 1.5 & \\
38	temperature$\times$item & 0.8 & \\
39	ordering$\times$item & 0.5 & \\
40	all others & $<$0.2 & \\
41	\bottomrule
42	\end{tabular}
43	\end{table}
44	
45	The per-model decomposition of interaction magnitude reveals meaningful asymmetry across eight models, suggesting that instruction-tuning strategies and pretraining data composition produce model-specific item difficulty profiles.
46	
47	\paragraph{D-study validation.} The eight-model sample validates the quasi-random model facet assumption. D-study projections from four-model subsets predicted $G = 0.893$ at eight models; the observed $G = 0.896$ confirms the projection ($\Delta = 0.003$).
48	
49	\subsection{Format-Conditional Variance Structure}

55	
56	\paragraph{Results.} Table~\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a systematic format-dependent pattern. For all three multiple-choice benchmarks, item difficulty is the dominant component (MMLU 38\%, ARC 38\%, HellaSwag 34\%), and model$\times$item interaction is the second largest. This structure is consistent with the cross-model analysis in Section~4.2.
57	
58	Free-form benchmarks exhibit a different variance profile. On GSM8K, item difficulty accounts for only 17\% of variance; model, prompt template, and their interaction become the leading components. MATH shows an intermediate pattern with item difficulty at 24\%, higher than GSM8K but well below the MC benchmarks.
59	
60	\begin{table}[t]
61	\centering
62	\caption{Format-conditional variance structure across five benchmarks (exp-002, 8 models, 2,304,000 records). Item difficulty (\%) per benchmark shown; MC = multiple-choice, FF = free-form.}
63	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}
64	\footnotesize
65	\begin{tabular}{l rrr rr}
66	\toprule
67	 & \multicolumn{3}{c}{MC} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{FF} \\
68	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-6}
69	 & MMLU & ARC & Hella. & GSM & MATH \\
70	\midrule
71	\textbf{item} & \textbf{38} & \textbf{38} & \textbf{34} & 17 & 24 \\
72	mod.$\times$item & 33 & 32 & 34 & 8 & 12 \\
73	\textbf{model} & 1 & 1 & 1 & 15 & 10 \\
74	prompt & $<$1 & $<$1 & $<$1 & 12 & 8 \\
75	mod.$\times$prmpt & $<$1 & $<$1 & $<$1 & 9 & 6 \\
76	residual & 23 & 22 & 25 & 30 & 32 \\
77	\bottomrule
78	\end{tabular}
79	\end{table}
80	
81	The variance structure is format-conditional: MC benchmarks concentrate variance in items (34--38\%), while FF benchmarks distribute it more evenly across model, prompt, and item facets (12--24\% each). This structural difference, however, does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag requires 171 items for $G \geq 0.80$, the highest of all benchmarks, demonstrating that format alone does not predict evaluation cost. Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the structural contrast.

_source line 1363 · 2026-05-22T00:28:19.436963+00:00_

1	Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks, totaling 2.3 million evaluation records. The variance structure is format-conditional: on multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks, model, prompt, and item each contribute 12--24\% with no single dominant source. Model$\times$item interaction (up to 33\%) is the hidden second-largest component, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\% interaction). Multi-facet designs require 10.0$\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to reach a generalizability coefficient of 0.80 (55 versus 552 items). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and Decision study (D-study) budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.

_source line 1365 · 2026-05-22T00:28:19.588478+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	This study establishes that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-conditional. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH), model, prompt, and item each contribute 12--24\% with no single dominant source. Model$\times$item interaction (up to 33\%) is the hidden second-largest source: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our cross-benchmark D-study framework extends the single-benchmark G-theory approach of \citet{mesting2026hidden} to format-conditional comparison and actionable design budgets.
5	
6	The format-conditional structure implies distinct optimization strategies. For multiple-choice benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; varying prompts or seeds contributes negligible variance. For free-form benchmarks, prompt design and model selection matter as much as item selection, and evaluation protocols should systematically vary both. Across formats, multi-facet designs are 10.0$\times$ more efficient than single-condition designs (55 versus 552 items for $G \geq 0.80$). BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring on single-GPU inference, which eliminates precision as a required control variable. The released D-study calculator enables practitioners to determine the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.
7	
8	Two extensions are planned. Multi-GPU tensor parallelism will test whether precision separability holds under distributed numerical non-associativity. Fine-tuning variance analysis will quantify how adaptation reshapes the item difficulty profiles that drive model$\times$item interaction.

The gap between MC item dominance (34--38\\%) and free-form item shares (17--24\\%) reflects a systematic format-dependent pattern in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection matter as much as item selection. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (up to 33\\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined.",
  "new_string": "All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 34--38\\% of total variance, with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source (30--36\\%). Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) exhibit a different profile: item, model, model$\\times$item, and model$\\times$prompt each contribute 5--17\\%, with no single dominant source and larger residual variance (40--44\\%). The gap between MC item dominance (34--38\\%) and free-form item shares (17--24\\%) reflects a systematic format-dependent pattern in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, model identity and model$\\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined."
}
```

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/experiments.tex:21:\paragraph{Results.} Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} presents the variance decomposition. Introducing model as a facet reshapes the structure: item difficulty drops from 58.4\% to 37.9\%, while model$\times$item interaction emerges as the second-largest component at up to 36\%. The model main effect accounts for 3--5\% on MC benchmarks and 9--11\% on free-form benchmarks, confirming that models differ primarily in \emph{which items} they find difficult rather than in overall ability level.

_source line 1403 · 2026-05-22T00:30:16.169668+00:00_

4	\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}
5	Factorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\%, with prompt$\times$judge interaction at 12.4\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across five benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.
6

_source line 1415 · 2026-05-22T00:31:09.923649+00:00_

50	The preceding analyses use only MMLU, a multiple-choice benchmark. We now test whether the item-dominated variance structure generalizes across evaluation formats by extending to five benchmarks spanning two response formats.
51	
52	\paragraph{Design.} The expanded design (exp-002) crosses 8 models $\times$ 5 benchmarks $\times$ BF16 $\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\times$ 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 4 orderings $\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 2,304,000 records. The benchmarks include three multiple-choice tasks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag) and two free-form generation tasks (GSM8K, MATH). The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\sigma^2_{\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance.
53	
54	\paragraph{Results.} Table~\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a systematic format-dependent pattern. For MMLU and ARC-Challenge, item difficulty is the dominant non-residual component (37.9\% each), with model$\times$item interaction as the second largest (30.2\% and 33.7\%). HellaSwag reverses this ordering: model$\times$item (36.0\%) exceeds item difficulty (34.3\%), indicating that models disagree most sharply on HellaSwag items. This structure is consistent with the cross-model analysis in Section~4.2.
55	
56	Free-form benchmarks exhibit a different variance profile. On GSM8K, variance distributes across item (16.9\%), model$\times$item (14.6\%), model$\times$prompt (12.9\%), and model (10.7\%), with no single dominant source; prompt main effect is only 5.0\%. MATH shows an intermediate pattern: item difficulty leads at 24.0\%, followed by model$\times$item (17.3\%) and model (8.5\%), closer to the MC structure but with a substantially larger residual (43.9\%).
57

_source line 1417 · 2026-05-22T00:32:04.939630+00:00_

1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We apply the G-theory framework from Section~3 to a fully crossed evaluation design spanning multiple models, benchmarks, and methodological facets. All experiments use open-weight 7--9B parameter models evaluated with vLLM~0.9.2 on NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs (96GB). Binary correctness (1/0) serves as the primary outcome measure throughout; generation-based scoring avoids the log-likelihood artifacts identified by \citet{hua2025flaw}.
4	
5	\subsection{Single-Model Baseline}
6	
7	We first establish the variance structure within a single model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) on MMLU to isolate methodological facet contributions before introducing cross-model variation.
8	
9	\paragraph{Design.} The G-study crosses six facets: 200 items $\times$ 3 numerical precisions (BF16, FP16, FP32) $\times$ 6 prompt templates $\times$ 6 random seeds $\times$ 3 temperature levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) $\times$ 4 answer orderings, yielding 259,200 binary observations. Variance components are estimated via Henderson Method~I with bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 resamples).
10	
11	\paragraph{Results.} Under binary scoring, item difficulty (\texttt{item\_id}) accounts for 58.4\% of total variance, with the residual term contributing 23.1\%. All main facet effects are negligible ($<$1\% each): precision 0.9\%, prompt template, temperature, seed, and ordering each $<$0.5\%. The interesting structure appears in item-conditional interactions: prompt$\times$item is the largest at 5.5\%, followed by seed$\times$item (4.7\%), precision$\times$item (3.3\%), temperature$\times$item (2.9\%), and ordering$\times$item (0.8\%). This pattern indicates that methodological choices do not shift performance uniformly across items; instead, specific items are differentially sensitive to specific facets---prompt template variation produces the largest item-conditional effect.
12

On GSM8K, variance distributes across item (16.9\\%), model$\\times$item (14.6\\%), model$\\times$prompt (12.9\\%), and model (10.7\\%); prompt main effect is only 5.0\\%. MATH shows an intermediate pattern: item leads at 24.0\\% with model$\\times$item at 17.3\\%, but residual variance is substantially larger (43.9\\%)."
  }
}

[Director 指令] [Step 5] Fig 1 已下载到 `artifacts/exp004/figures/fig1_variance_decomposition.{pdf,png}`，放入论文后启动 Step 5 Iterative Refinement。

**Fig 1 放置**：替换旧图，更新 caption。Reviewer 发现右上角图例与 "Free-Form" 标签重叠——如果你能用 LaTeX 调位置就调，否则标记给 Agent 重绘。

**Step 5 目标**：全文通读打磨，确保所有数字、叙事、claim 一致。重点检查：

1. **数字一致性**：Table 2 ↔ Table 3 ↔ body text ↔ Abstract ↔ Conclusion 的所有数字必须完全匹配
2. **三层叙事一致性**：确保论文不会在 item budget 维度 overclaim（HellaSwag MC 需要最多 items）
3. **GSM8K 描述**：prompt 主效应只有 5%，不是 12%——确保全文没有残留 "prompt 12%" 的旧表述
4. **model main effect**：MC 3-5%, FF 9-11%——确保没有残留 "below 1%"
5. **Fig 1 caption**：mention 8 models, 5 benchmarks, Henderson Method I, HellaSwag reversal (model×item > item)
6. **页数**：AAAI 2027 target，确认 main text ≤ 8 pages

完成后自行提交 Reviewer 审查（paper_agent_request_review）。如果 Reviewer 没有 critical，报告给我准备 submit_paper_to_review。

14	---
15	
16	# Step 5 — Iterative Refinement
17	
18	Step 4 全部章节落盘后，Paper Agent 进入 Step 5：把全文递交 Reviewer 评分，按反馈修改，直到收敛。
19	
20	**核心约束**：
21	- 串行——一轮 Reviewer 返回结果处理完才进下一轮，禁止并发请求
22	- 评分驱动——overall 提升才 accept 本轮，下降则 git revert
23	- 上限 5 轮——硬约束，达到上限即停，不论分数
24	
25	## 状态记录（git 是 truth source）
26	
27	每轮修改前 commit 当前论文目录，作为 revert 锚点。无需额外 state 文件。
28	
29	```
30	git -C projects/{project} add paper/
31	git -C projects/{project} commit -m "[paper] refinement round {N-1}"
32	```
33	
34	Round N 的 commit message 用 `N-1`，即 round 1 修改前 commit `round 0`（Step 4 落盘后的初稿）。
35	
36	## 单轮流程
37	
38	```
39	Round N（N = 1..5）：
40	
41	1. git commit 当前版本（message: "[paper] refinement round N-1"）
42	   └── 落地评分下降时的 revert 锚点
43	
44	2. 组装审查请求：
45	   - read_project_file("paper_checklist.yaml") — 用户自定义必查项
46	   - 拼 LaTeX 全文（或 Patch 模式下的差异）+ writing_plan.yaml 路径
47	   - 末尾追加 "## 用户自定义必查项"（见下方"checklist 注入格式"）
48	
49	3. paper_agent_request_review(text=组装后的请求, project=...)
50	   └── 请求末尾追加维度提醒："除标准的 Originality/Clarity/Soundness/Significance/Presentation 维度外，请额外检查：叙事一致性（各章节是否讲同一个故事）、贡献-范围对齐（Intro contribution ↔ 正文 section 1:1）、去 AI 风格（em-dash、-ing、filler words）。参考 reviewer-audit-checklist §7。"
51	   └── Reviewer 通过 reviewer_post_to_director 回传评分
52	
53	4. 解析 Reviewer 响应：
54	   - 维度分（Originality/Clarity/Soundness/Significance/Presentation，1-4）
55	   - overall（1-10）
56	   - 各条具体问题 severity（critical/warning/info）
57	   - checklist 项 PASS/FAIL
58	   ★ 任一 checklist FAIL → 该条问题 severity 自动按 critical 对待
59	
60	5. 按问题类型分流处理：
61	   - 事实性错误（数字不一致 / 引用错位）→ 自己用 edit_paper_section 改
62	   - 段落级叙事问题（逻辑断裂 / 强度词超限）→ spawn Worker 重写段落，回来再 edit_paper_section
63	   - 结构问题（章节顺序 / 段落归并）→ 自己用 edit_paper_section
64	   - critical 且 claim 
…[truncated 447 chars]

1	---
2	name: paper-quality-lessons
3	description: NeurIPS 投稿实战经验总结——人工审查中提炼的写作规范、审查清单、常见反模式。Paper Agent / Reviewer / Director 写论文或审查论文时加载。
4	allowed-tools: [Read, Write, Edit, Glob, Grep]

6	version: 1
7	tags:
8	  - writing
9	  - paper
10	  - quality

12	  - review

14	  - agent
15	  - reviewer
16	  - director
17	  - paper_agent
18	---
19	
20	# 论文写作质量指南（NeurIPS 实战经验）
21	
22	> 来源：2026 年 NeurIPS 投稿期间，从 20+ 项目的人工审查反馈中提炼。
23	> 每条规则都源自真实的 bug / 退回 / 审稿质疑。
24	
25	---
26	
27	## 一、数据诚信与溯源（最高优先级）
28	
29	### 1.1 数字必须有源头
30	
31	- 论文中**每一个数字**必须可追溯到 registry 实验 ID 或原始结果文件
32	- 禁止凭记忆写数字——必须 `cat result.json | head` + `ls -la` 附上文件证据
33	- 计算值（均值、标准差、DID、delta）必须独立重算，不信 Agent 第一手汇报
34	- `paper_provenance.yaml` 覆盖率必须 100%，改数字必须同步更新 provenance
35	
36	> **教训（D149 事件）**：Agent 曾伪造完整的 metrics 数据包括 p-value，事后被 Reviewer 独立 parse JSON 抓出。从此所有关键数据必须 Reviewer 独立验证到字段级别。
37	
38	### 1.2 不同来源数据不能混用
39	
40	- 配对数据必须同 seed、同 c 值、同 N、同 evaluation protocol
41	- 两张表如果 protocol 不同（如 0-shot vs 8-shot），论文必须明确说明
42	- 跨 config 比较 delta 会引入系统误差——必须补跑同 config baseline 后才能比较
43	- 非等量对比必须显式标注（如 "at unmatched volumes"）
44	
45	> **教训（D027 事件）**：不同 config 文件的 baseline 不同，跨 config 比较 delta 值引入了 artifact。
46	
47	### 1.3 结论撤回后不可复活
48	
49	- 被 Director shelved 的数据（如因截断混淆），禁止重新引入论文
50	- `paper_provenance.yaml` 中 `verified: false` 的 claim，不得恢复
51	- 术语修正后不可回退（如 "same norm" → "same per-layer standard deviation"）
52	
53	---
54	
55	## 二、Claim 强度与数据匹配
56	
57	### 2.1 Claim 强度规则
58	
59	| 数据支撑 | 允许的最强表述 |
60	|----------|--------------|
61	| 单 seed, 单数据集 | "preliminary observation", "initial evidence" |
62	| 单 seed, 多数据集 | "suggestive pattern", "indicative trend" |
63	| 多 seed, 单数据集 | 报告均值±标准差，可写 "we find" |
64	| 多 seed, 多数据集 | 可用 "robust", "consistent" |
65	| 统计检验显著 | 可用 "significant", 附 p-value |
66	| 单 seed + 外推到其他条件 | 必须加限定，不能声称 "scale-invariant" 等 |
67	
68	### 2.2 禁止的过强表述
69	
70	- "remarkably stable" → 改为 "relatively stable" 或给出量化波动范围
71	- "confirmed" → 仅 "suggestive" 级别数据不能用
72	- "
…[truncated 366 chars]

_source line 1496 · 2026-05-22T00:58:29.995310+00:00_

1	Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks, totaling 2.3 million evaluation records. The variance structure is format-conditional: on multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks, item, model, and their interactions each contribute 5--24\% with no single dominant source. Model$\times$item interaction (up to 36\%) is the hidden second-largest component, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\% interaction). Multi-facet designs require 10.0$\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to reach a generalizability coefficient of 0.80 (55 versus 552 items). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and Decision study (D-study) budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	On multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 34--38\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$3\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, the structure shifts: model$\times$prompt interaction alone accounts for 13\%, while item selection drops to 17\%. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 2.3 million evaluation records across five benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.
5	
6	Existing studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decompo
…[truncated 4475 chars]

_source line 1500 · 2026-05-22T00:58:31.001312+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	This study establishes that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-conditional. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH), item, model, and their interactions each contribute 5--24\% with no single dominant source. Model$\times$item interaction (up to 36\%) is the hidden second-largest source: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our cross-benchmark D-study framework extends the single-benchmark G-theory approach of \citet{mesting2026hidden} to format-conditional comparison and actionable design budgets.
5	
6	The format-conditional structure implies distinct optimization strategies. For multiple-choice benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; varying prompts or seeds contributes negligible variance. For free-form benchmarks, model identity and model$\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection, and evaluation protocols should systematically vary both. Across formats, multi-facet designs are 10.0$\times$ more efficient than single-condition designs (55 versus 552 items for $G \geq 0.80$). BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring on single-GPU inference, which eliminates precision as a required control variable. The released D-study calculator enables practitioners to determine the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.
7	
8	Two extensions are planned. Multi-GPU tensor parallelism will test whether precision separability holds under distributed numerical non-associativity. Fine-tuning variance analysis will quantify how adaptation reshapes the item difficulty profiles that drive model$\times$item interaction.

1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We apply the G-theory framework from Section~3 to a fully crossed evaluation design spanning multiple models, benchmarks, and methodological facets. All experiments use open-weight 7--9B parameter models evaluated with vLLM~0.9.2 on NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs (96GB). Binary correctness (1/0) serves as the primary outcome measure throughout; generation-based scoring avoids the log-likelihood artifacts identified by \citet{hua2025flaw}.
4	
5	\subsection{Single-Model Baseline}
6	
7	We first establish the variance structure within a single model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) on MMLU to isolate methodological facet contributions before introducing cross-model variation.
8	
9	\paragraph{Design.} The G-study crosses six facets: 200 items $\times$ 3 numerical precisions (BF16, FP16, FP32) $\times$ 6 prompt templates $\times$ 6 random seeds $\times$ 3 temperature levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) $\times$ 4 answer orderings, yielding 259,200 binary observations. Variance components are estimated via Henderson Method~I with bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 resamples).
10	
11	\paragraph{Results.} Under binary scoring, item difficulty (\texttt{item\_id}) accounts for 58.4\% of total variance, with the residual term contributing 23.1\%. All main facet effects are negligible ($<$1\% each): precision 0.9\%, prompt template, temperature, seed, and ordering each $<$0.5\%. The interesting structure appears in item-conditional interactions: prompt$\times$item is the largest at 5.5\%, followed by seed$\times$item (4.7\%), precision$\times$item (3.3\%), temperature$\times$item (2.9\%), and ordering$\times$item (0.8\%). This pattern indicates that methodological choices do not shift performance uniformly across items; instead, specific items are differentially sensitive to specific facets---prompt template variation produces the largest item-conditional effect.
12

…[truncated 12304 chars]

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}
5	Factorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\%, with prompt$\times$judge interaction at 12.4\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across five benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.
6	
7	\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}
8	A parallel line of work has examined individual variance sources in isolation. On the infrastructure side, \citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\% accuracy variation from numerical precision and GPU type but provided no factorial attribution. \citet{messina2026background} formalized the concept of background temperature from floating-point non-associativity in GPU computation, and \citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} proposed batch-invariant kernels to mitigate C
…[truncated 3498 chars]

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Generalizability Theory}
5	\label{sec:gtheory}
6	
7	Generalizability theory (G-theory) treats each measurement as one observation from a universe of admissible conditions defined by the crossed facets of an evaluation design \cite{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability}. The \emph{object of measurement} is the entity whose consistency is of interest. In our setting, test items ($p$) serve as the object of measurement, and the goal is to determine how reliably item-level scores generalize across the methodological conditions under which they are collected.
8	
9	A \emph{G-study} decomposes total observed variance into components $\sigma^2_x$ for each facet $x$ and their interactions. Each component quantifies how much variability that source contributes to the overall measurement. Large interaction components (e.g., $\sigma^2_{pM}$ for item-by-model) indicate that facets do not affect items uniformly but instead create differential difficulty profiles. We estimate variance components via Henderson Method~I, an unbiased ANOVA-based estimator for balanced designs \cite[Ch.~3]{brennan2001generalizability}, with 1,000-resample bootstrap confidence intervals \cite{li2023bootstrap}.
10	
11	A \emph{D-study} projects the generalizability coefficient under hypothetical designs with different numbers of conditions per facet:
12	\begin{equation}
13	G_\text{item} = \frac{\sigma^2_p}{\sigma^2_p + \sigma^2_\text{rel}}
14	\label{eq:gcoeff}
15	\end{equation}
16	where $\sigma^2_\text{rel}$ aggregates all item-by-facet interaction variances, each divided by the number of conditions for the corresponding facets \cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}. This prospective capability distinguishes G-theory from classical ANOVA: rather than only attributing variance post hoc, a D-study enables optimization of evaluation designs \emph{before} data collection \cite{shavelson1989gtheory}. Throughout this paper, we use
…[truncated 8379 chars]

1	\appendix
2	\section{Additional Robustness Analyses}
3	\label{app:robustness}
4	
5	\textbf{Estimator robustness.} REML and Henderson Method~I differ by $<$0.5 percentage points on all components.
6	
7	\textbf{CI method.} Bootstrap and jackknife confidence intervals agree closely (width ratio 0.96--1.04 across components).
8	
9	\textbf{Facet specification.} Treating facets as fixed vs.\ random changes $G_\text{item}$ by only 0.37 percentage points.
10	
11	\textbf{Leave-one-model-out (LOMO).} All three-model subsets preserve the item + model$\times$item dominance pattern (item\_id 39--45\%, model$\times$item 32--37\%, $G_\text{item}$ 0.747--0.793).
12	
13	\textbf{Null calibration.} A Bernoulli simulation with matched item difficulties produces a variance ratio of 1.0008 relative to observed data, confirming that the decomposition does not introduce systematic bias on binary outcomes.
14

{
  "text": "## Step 5 Round 1 — Full Paper Review Request\n\n**Project**: nlp_variance_gtheory\n**Venue target**: AAAI 2027 (8-page main text limit)\n**Writing plan**: projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/writing_plan.yaml\n\n### 审查重点\n\n1. **数字一致性**：以下核心数字必须在 Abstract / Intro / Experiments / Conclusion 完全一致：\n   - MC item range: 34--38% (Table 3: MMLU 37.9, ARC 37.9, HellaSwag 34.3)\n   - FF component range: 5--24% (GSM8K prompt 5.0% to MATH item 24.0%)\n   - model×item: up to 36% (HellaSwag 36.0%)\n   - 10.0× efficiency: 55 vs 552 items for G≥0.80\n   - 2.3M records, 8 models, 5 benchmarks\n\n2. **GSM8K 描述**：prompt 主效应 5.0%（不是 12%）。全文不应有残留的 \"prompt 12%\" 表述。GSM8K 的 leading components: item 16.9%, model×item 14.6%, model×prompt 12.9%, model 10.7%。\n\n3. **model main effect**：MC 3-5% (Table 3: 2.8-5.4%), FF 9-11% (Table 3: 8.5-10.7%)。全文不应有残留的 \"below 1%\"。\n\n4. **HellaSwag reversal**：model×item (36.0%) > item (34.3%)。Fig 1 caption 和 §4.3 body text 必须明确提到。\n\n5. **叙事一致性**：\n   - 三层叙事（Abstract=headline → Intro=full story → Experiments=evidence）讲的是同一个故事\n   - 不在 item budget 维度 overclaim（HellaSwag MC 需要 171 items 最多，format 不预测 cost）\n   - Conclusion 的优化建议与实验证据匹配\n\n6. **页数**：main text ≤ 8 pages（当前 7 页 main text + references + appendix = 9 total PDF pages）\n\n7. **Fig 1 caption**：应提到 8 models, 5 benchmarks, Henderson Method I, HellaSwag reversal\n\n8. **Appendix LOMO**：已从 \"three-model\" 修正为 \"seven-model\"。LOMO 数值范围 (item_id 39-45%, model×item 32-37%, G 0.747-0.793) 可能是 4-model era 残留——请检查是否与 8-model 设计 plausible。\n\n### LaTeX 全文\n\n**abstract.tex**:\n```latex\nLarge Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks, totaling 2.3 million evaluation records. The variance structure is format-conditional: on multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks, item, model, and their interactions each contribute 5--24\\% with no single dominant source. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest component, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\\% interaction). Multi-facet designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to reach a generalizability coefficient of 0.80 (55 versus 552 items). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and Decision study (D-study) budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.\n```\n\n**introduction.tex**: (7 paragraphs, see file)\n- Opening hook: MC 34-38% item / <3% prompt vs GSM8K model×prompt 13% / item 17%\n- Gap analysis: three gaps (a)(b)(c)\n- Central finding: format-conditional, 34-38% MC, 5-24% FF, model×item up to 36%\n- 4 contributions: format-conditional structure, precision separability, design-dependent budgets, open-source pipeline\n\n**method.tex**: (6 subsections)\n- G-theory framework, Henderson Method I, D-study formula\n- Exp-001: 7 facets × MMLU, 460,800 BF16 cross-model, 259,200 single-model\n- Exp-002: 5 benchmarks, 2,304,000 records\n- Evaluation protocol, binary data assumptions, model-as-facet justification\n\n**experiments.tex**: (6 subsections)\n- §4.1 Single-model: item 58.4%, G=0.936, text-exact-match collapse\n- §4.2 Cross-model: Table 2 (37.9/30.2/25.0/2.8/2.5/1.6), model×item up to 36%, model 3-5% MC / 9-11% FF\n- §4.3 Format-conditional: Table 3 (5 benchmarks × 6 components), HellaSwag reversal, GSM8K distributed, MATH intermediate, within-MMLU gradient ρ=0.806\n- §4.4 Precision: BF16≈FP16, <0.1% interaction\n- §4.5 D-study: Table 4 (55/552/10.0×), IRT G=0.457\n- §4.6 Robustness: item resampling CV=3.1%, 5 checks in appendix\n\n**related_work.tex**: 5 paragraphs positioning vs Mesting, IRT, isolated-factor studies\n\n**conclusion.tex**: format-conditional summary, optimization strategies, two extensions planned\n\n**limitations.tex**: 3 constraints (model scope, single-GPU, benchmark coverage)\n\n**appendix.tex**: 5 robustness checks (estimator, CI, facet spec, LOMO, null calibration)\n\n### 额外审查维度\n\n除标准的 Originality/Clarity/Soundness/Significance/Presentation 维度外，请额外检查：\n- 叙事一致性（各章节是否讲同一个故事）\n- 贡献-范围对齐（Intro contribution ↔ 正文 section 1:1）\n- 去 AI 风格（em-dash、-ing、filler words）\n- Claim 强度与 evidence 匹配（单数据集/多数据集/统计检验）",
  "project": "nlp_variance_gtheory"
}
````

## C5 Table 3 加 "all others" 行

## W1 Table 2 CI 列
CI 列全空。服务器 JSON 有 bootstrap CI 值（如 item_id [30.37, 45.32]）。两个选择：

## W5 补上 permutation test 结果

_source line 1544 · 2026-05-22T01:24:21.224536+00:00_

79	
80	The variance structure is format-conditional: MC benchmarks concentrate variance in items and model$\times$item interaction (34--38\% and 30--36\%), while FF benchmarks distribute it across item, model, and interaction components (5--24\% each) with larger residuals (40--44\%). This structural difference does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag requires 171 items for $G \geq 0.80$, the highest of all benchmarks, showing that format alone does not predict evaluation cost. Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast.
81	
82	To probe the mechanism behind item variance, we exploit the natural heterogeneity within MMLU's 57 subjects. Subjects with higher item difficulty dispersion (measured by $\text{std}(p_i)$ across models) exhibit higher item variance shares, with Spearman $\rho = 0.806$ ($p = 0.005$, $n = 10$ subjects). Notably, \texttt{high\_school\_mathematics}, the only MC subject with open-ended numerical items, shows item variance of just 10.4\%---below the free-form benchmark GSM8K (16.9\%)---suggesting that item heterogeneity, not format per se, drives variance allocation.
83	
84	\begin{figure}[t]

Cohen's $d = 1.73$ [1.72, 1.83] and the 95\\% CI for the mean difference is [11.44, 21.02] percentage points, confirming a large effect despite the constrained $p$-value. This structural difference does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag requires 171 items for $G \\geq 0.80$, the highest of all benchmarks, showing that format alone does not predict evaluation cost. ## C1/C2: GSM8K model×prompt = 8.5%（不是 12.9%）

12.9% 是错误的（把 prompt:item 3.06% 等吸收进了 model:prompt）。

修正：
- Table 3 GSM8K model×prompt: 12.9% → **8.5%**
- Table 3 GSM8K prompt: 5.0% → **5.4%**  
- Table 3 GSM8K "all others" 行需重算（之前加的 <1.0 也可能不对了）
- Introduction hook: "model×prompt interaction alone accounts for 13%" → **"approximately 9%"**
- Body text §4.3 中引用 GSM8K model×prompt 的地方同步改为 8.5%
- Abstract/Conclusion 如有引用也改

GSM8K 完整方差分解（权威值）：
| Component | % |
|-----------|---|
| item_id | 16.86 |
| model:item_id | 14.59 |
| model_id | 10.68 |
| model:prompt | 8.53 |
| prompt | 5.41 |
| prompt:item | 3.06 |
| residual | 39.87 |

论文写 "432 conditions (6 prompts × 6 seeds × 3 temperatures × 4 orderings)"，实际是 **1,296 conditions (3 precisions × 3 temperatures × 6 prompts × 6 seeds × 4 orderings)**。漏了 3 precisions。

修正：experiments.tex 中 D-study 描述改为 "1,296 conditions per item (3 precisions × 3 temperatures × 6 prompts × 6 seeds × 4 orderings)"。这是 exp-001 MMLU 单模型设计的完整条件数。

**10.0× ratio、55/552 items、G=0.936/0.592 这些数字不变。**

## C4: Appendix LOMO 更新

旧值（4-model 时代，已过时）：G_item 0.747-0.793, item_id 39-45%
新值（8-model LOMO，从 lomo_8model_mmlu.json）：

**G_item [0.877, 0.889], item_id [36.4%, 40.9%]**

Appendix LOMO 段落改为：
"All eight seven-model subsets preserve the item + model×item dominance pattern (item\_id 36--41\%, $G_\text{item}$ 0.877--0.889), confirming that the variance structure is robust to model composition."

## 完成后

以上全部改完后重新编译，自行通读确认无残留的旧值（12.9%, 432, 0.747 等），然后提交 Reviewer Round 2。

56	Free-form benchmarks differ markedly. On GSM8K, variance distributes across item (16.9\%), model$\times$item (14.6\%), model$\times$prompt (12.9\%), and model (10.7\%); prompt main effect is only 5.0\%. MATH shows an intermediate pattern: item leads at 24.0\% with model$\times$item at 17.3\%, but residual variance is substantially larger (43.9\%).
57

80	The variance structure is format-conditional: MC benchmarks concentrate variance in items and model$\times$item interaction (34--38\% and 30--36\%), while FF benchmarks distribute it across item, model, and interaction components (5--24\% each) with larger residuals (40--44\%). A permutation test of the MC versus FF item-variance difference yields $p = 0.10$ ($n = 5$ benchmarks, $\binom{5}{3} = 10$ exhaustive permutations), the theoretical minimum for this sample size. Cohen's $d = 1.73$ [1.72, 1.83] and the 95\% CI for the mean difference is [11.44, 21.02] percentage points, confirming a large effect despite the constrained $p$-value. This structural differe

_source line 1562 · 2026-05-22T01:29:13.932223+00:00_

98	
99	\subsection{Design-Dependent Reproducibility Budgets}
100	
101	The previous sections establish that item selection and model$\times$item interaction dominate evaluation variance. A natural question follows: how many items does a given evaluation design need to achieve a target reliability? We address this through D-study projections that translate variance components into concrete item budgets.
102	
103	Table~\ref{tab:dstudy} compares four evaluation designs at the $G \geq 0.80$ threshold. The multi-facet design (432 conditions per item: 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 3 temperatures $\times$ 4 orderings) achieves $G = 0.936$ at 200 items and requires only 55 items for $G \geq 0.80$. A single-condition design (one fixed prompt, seed, ordering, and precision per item) reaches only $G = 0.592$ at 200 items and requires 552 items for the same threshold. The ratio between these budgets is 10.0$\times$, and this ratio remains stable across subset sizes from 50 to 200 items (CV = 1.9\%).
104	
105	\begin{table}[t]
106	\centering
107	\caption{D-study reproducibility budgets under different evaluation designs. The multi-facet design requires 10.0$\times$ fewer items than single-condition evaluation to reach $G \geq 0.80$.}

[Director 指令] [Patch] Step 5 Round 2 修复 — 2 个 WARNING

## W1: Table 3 "prompt" 行语义不一致

当前 Table 3 的 "prompt" 行在不同 benchmark 代表不同组件：
- MMLU: 2.5% = prompt×item（prompt 主效应 <1%）
- GSM8K: 5.4% = prompt 主效应（prompt×item = 3.06%）

修正方案：**统一为 prompt 主效应**，prompt×item 归入 "all others"。

各 benchmark prompt 主效应权威值：
- MMLU: <1%（≈0.2%，归入 all others 也行，或显示 <1）
- ARC: 0.4%（已正确）
- HellaSwag: 2.3%（检查这是主效应还是 prompt×item）
- GSM8K: 5.4%（已正确）
- MATH: 1.0%（检查）

如果难以确认各 benchmark 的 prompt 主效应 vs prompt×item，备选方案：在 Table 3 caption 加脚注 "Prompt = prompt template main effect for GSM8K and MATH; prompt×item for MC benchmarks where prompt main effect is negligible (<1%)"。

## W4: Cohen's d CI 需说明来源

当前 §4.3: "Cohen's d=1.73 [1.72, 1.83]"

CI [1.72, 1.83] 来自 item-level bootstrap（200 items/benchmark），不是 benchmark-level 不确定性（n=5 的解析 CI 远更宽）。审稿人可能误解为 benchmark-level precision。

修正：改为 "Cohen's $d = 1.73$ (bootstrap CI over item resampling: [1.72, 1.83]; note that benchmark-level generalization uncertainty is larger given $n = 5$)"

或者更简洁：删除 CI，只报告点估计 + n=5 限制。推荐后者——CI 在这个 context 下容易误导。

## 不需要改的
- W2: 模板是 AAAI 不是 EMNLP，aaai2026 暂时可用
- W3: 你上轮确认图片已 cp 重命名编译正常——如果确实没问题不用改
- W5: 废弃文件清理可以之后做

改完提交 Reviewer Round 3。

58	\begin{table}[t]
59	\centering
60	\caption{Format-conditional variance structure across five benchmarks (exp-002, 8 models, 2,304,000 records). Variance components (\%) per benchmark; MC = multiple-choice, FF = free-form. Bold indicates the dominant non-residual component per benchmark.}
61	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}
62	\scriptsize
63	\begin{tabular}{l rrr rr}
64	\toprule
65	 & \multicolumn{3}{c}{MC} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{FF} \\
66	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-6}
67	 & MMLU & ARC & Hella. & GSM & MATH \\
68	\midrule
69	\textbf{item} & \textbf{37.9} & \textbf{37.9} & 34.3 & 16.9 & 24.0 \\
70	mod.$\times$item & 30.2 & 33.7 & \textbf{36.0} & 14.6 & 17.3 \\
71	model & 2.8 & 3.1 & 5.4 & 10.7 & 8.5 \\
72	prompt & 2.5 & 0.4 & 2.3 & 5.4 & 1.0 \\
73	mod.$\times$prmpt & 1.6 & 8.0 & 0.6 & 8.5 & 2.0 \\
74	residual & 25.0 & 15.4 & 21.4 & 39.9 & 43.9 \\
75	all others & $<$1.0 & 1.5 & $<$1.0 & 4.0 & 3.3 \\
76	\bottomrule
77	\end{tabular}
78	\end{table}
79	
80	The variance structure is format-conditional: MC benchmarks concentrate variance in items and model$\times$item interaction (34--38\% and 30--36\%), while FF benchmarks distribute it across item, model, and interaction components (5--24\% each) with larger residuals (40--44\%). A permutation test of the MC versus FF item-variance difference yields $p = 0.10$ ($n = 5$ benchmarks, $\binom{5}{3} = 10$ exhaustive permutations), the theoretical minimum for this sample size. Cohen's $d = 1.73$ [1.72, 1.83] and the 95\% CI for the mean difference is [11.44, 21.02] percentage points, confirming a large effect despite the constrained $p$-value. This structural difference does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag requires 171 items for $G \geq 0.80$, the highest of all benchmarks, showing that format alone does not predict evaluation cost. Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast.
81

_source line 1634 · 2026-05-22T03:04:53.113038+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Patch] HellaSwag n(G≥0.80) 数字修正 — 6-model → 8-model

论文中 HellaSwag "requires 171 items for G≥0.80, the highest of all benchmarks" 使用了 6-model 分析值。8-model 下实际是 118 items，且 GSM8K (128) 才是最高。

**8-model 各 benchmark n(G≥0.80)**：
| Benchmark | n(G≥0.80) | Format |
|-----------|-----------|--------|
| ARC | 98 | MC |
| MATH | 101 | FF |
| HellaSwag | 118 | MC |
| GSM8K | 128 | FF |

排序：ARC(MC) < MATH(FF) < HellaSwag(MC) < GSM8K(FF) — MC 和 FF 完全交错，format 无法预测 item budget。

**需要修正的地方**（全文搜索 "171"）：

1. experiments.tex §4.3：
旧："HellaSwag requires 171 items for G ≥ 0.80, the highest of all benchmarks, demonstrating that format alone does not predict evaluation cost"
新："GSM8K requires 128 items for $G \geq 0.80$---the highest of all benchmarks---while HellaSwag (MC, 118) exceeds both ARC (MC, 98) and MATH (FF, 101), demonstrating that format alone does not predict evaluation cost"

2. abstract/conclusion 如有引用 171 或 "highest"，同步修正

3. paper_framing.md 的 three-level narrative 第三层也需更新（我会处理）

这个修正实际上**强化**了叙事：MC/FF 完全交错 (98, 101, 118, 128) 比原来的 "HellaSwag 是例外" 更有说服力。

_source line 1640 · 2026-05-22T03:05:09.913876+00:00_

76	\bottomrule
77	\end{tabular}
78	\end{table}
79	
80	The variance structure is format-conditional: MC benchmarks concentrate variance in items and model$\times$item interaction (34--38\% and 30--36\%), while FF benchmarks distribute it across item, model, and interaction components (5--24\% each) with larger residuals (40--44\%). A permutation test of the MC versus FF item-variance difference yields $p = 0.10$ ($n = 5$ benchmarks, $\binom{5}{3} = 10$ exhaustive permutations), the theoretical minimum for this sample size. The effect size is large (Cohen's $d = 1.73$; mean difference 95\% CI [11.44, 21.02] percentage points), though benchmark-level generalization uncertainty is substantial given $n = 5$. This structural difference does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag requires 171 items for $G \geq 0.80$, the highest of all benchmarks, showing that format alone does not predict evaluation cost. Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast.
81	
82	To probe the mechanism behind item variance, we exploit the natural heterogeneity within MMLU's 57 subjects. Subjects with higher item difficulty dispersion (measured by $\text{std}(p_i)$ across models) exhibit higher item variance shares, with Spearman $\rho = 0.806$ ($p = 0.005$, $n = 10$ subjects). Notably, \texttt{high\_school\_mathematics}, the only MC subject with open-ended numerical items, shows item variance of just 10.4\%---below the free-form benchmark GSM8K (16.9\%)---suggesting that item heterogeneity, not format per se, drives variance allocation.
83	
84	\begin{figure}[t]

Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) distribute variance across item, model, and interaction components with no single dominant source.}

78	\end{table}
79	
80	The variance structure is format-conditional: MC benchmarks concentrate variance in items and model$\times$item interaction (34--38\% and 30--36\%), while FF benchmarks distribute it across item, model, and interaction components (5--24\% each) with larger residuals (40--44\%). A permutation test of the MC versus FF item-variance difference yields $p = 0.10$ ($n = 5$ benchmarks, $\binom{5}{3} = 10$ exhaustive permutations), the theoretical minimum for this sample size. The effect size is large (Cohen's $d = 1.73$; mean difference 95\% CI [11.44, 21.02] percentage points), though benchmark-level generalization uncertainty is substantial given $n = 5$. This structural difference does not extend to item budgets: GSM8K requires 128 items for $G \geq 0.80$---the highest of all benchmarks---while HellaSwag (MC, 118) exceeds both ARC (MC, 98) and MATH (FF, 101), demonstrating that format alone does not predict evaluation cost. Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast.
81	
82	To probe the mechanism behind item variance, we exploit the natural heterogeneity within MMLU's 57 subjects. Subjects with higher item difficulty dispersion (measured by $\text{std}(p_i)$ across models) exhibit higher item variance shares, with Spearman $\rho = 0.806$ ($p = 0.005$, $n = 10$ subjects). Notably, \texttt{high\_school\_mathematics}, the only MC subject with open-ended numerical items, shows item variance of just 10.4\%---below the free-form benchmark GSM8K (16.9\%)---suggesting that item heterogeneity, not format per se, drives variance allocation.

| Format | Benchmarks | Single/Multi ratio |

99	\subsection{Design-Dependent Reproducibility Budgets}
100	
101	The previous sections establish that item selection and model$\times$item interaction dominate evaluation variance. A natural question follows: how many items does a given evaluation design need to achieve a target reliability? We address this through D-study projections that translate variance components into concrete item budgets.
102	
103	Table~\ref{tab:dstudy} compares four evaluation designs at the $G \geq 0.80$ threshold. The multi-facet design (1,296 conditions per item: 3 precisions $\times$ 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 3 temperatures $\times$ 4 orderings) achieves $G = 0.936$ at 200 items and requires only 55 items for $G \geq 0.80$. A single-condition design (one fixed prompt, seed, ordering, and precision per item) reaches only $G = 0.592$ at 200 items and requires 552 items for the same threshold. The ratio between these budgets is 10.0$\times$, and this ratio remains stable across subset sizes from 50 to 200 items (CV = 1.9\%).
104

_source line 1662 · 2026-05-22T03:06:17.752698+00:00_

125	The comparison with IRT-based efficient benchmarking is instructive. \citet{zhou2026irt} use IRT to select 116 informative items from MMLU, optimizing for item discrimination. When we evaluate these 116 items under single-condition protocols, the resulting $G = 0.457$ falls well below the reliability threshold. This is not a failure of IRT item selection; rather, it reflects that IRT optimizes a different objective (latent trait estimation) that does not account for methodological facet variance. IRT selects items optimized for trait discrimination; multi-facet G-study improves reliability orthogonally by averaging over methodological noise sources that IRT does not model. Under the multi-facet design, 116 items also exceed $G = 0.80$, confirming that the reliability gap is driven by evaluation design, not item quality.
126	
127	The efficiency gap between multi-facet and single-condition designs is itself format-conditional. Per-benchmark D-study projections reveal that MC benchmarks exhibit a 10--12$\times$ ratio (MMLU 10.0$\times$, ARC 11.0$\times$, HellaSwag 11.9$\times$), while FF benchmarks show a 21--22$\times$ ratio (GSM8K 21.6$\times$, MATH 21.4$\times$). Single-condition evaluation of free-form benchmarks incurs approximately double the reliability penalty of multiple-choice benchmarks, making multi-facet designs even more critical for reasoning tasks where variance distributes across model, prompt, and interaction components (\S\ref{sec:format_conditional}).
128	
129	Figure~\ref{fig:dstudy} plots the D-study projection curves for each design. The multi-facet curve saturates rapidly (diminishing returns beyond $\sim$150 items), while the single-condition curve rises slowly and does not reach $G = 0.80$ within typical benchmark sizes.

_source line 1666 · 2026-05-22T03:06:33.522489+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	This study establishes that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-conditional. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH), item, model, and their interactions each contribute 5--24\% with no single dominant source. Model$\times$item interaction (up to 36\%) is the hidden second-largest source: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our cross-benchmark D-study framework extends the single-benchmark G-theory approach of \citet{mesting2026hidden} to format-conditional comparison and actionable design budgets.
5	
6	The format-conditional structure implies distinct optimization strategies. For multiple-choice benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; varying prompts or seeds contributes negligible variance. For free-form benchmarks, model identity and model$\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection, and evaluation protocols should systematically vary both. Across formats, multi-facet designs are 10.0$\times$ more efficient than single-condition designs (55 versus 552 items for $G \geq 0.80$). BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring on single-GPU inference, which eliminates precision as a required control variable. The released D-study calculator enables practitioners to determine the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.
7	
8	Two extensions are planned. Multi-GPU tensor parallelism will test whether precision separability holds under distributed numerical non-associativity. Fine-tuning variance analysis will quantify how adaptation reshapes the item difficulty profiles that drive model$\times$item interaction.

_source line 1668 · 2026-05-22T03:06:36.231662+00:00_

1	Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks, totaling 2.3 million evaluation records. The variance structure is format-conditional: on multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks, item, model, and their interactions each contribute 5--24\% with no single dominant source. Model$\times$item interaction (up to 36\%) is the hidden second-largest component, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\% interaction). Multi-facet designs require 10.0$\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to reach a generalizability coefficient of 0.80 (55 versus 552 items). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and Decision study (D-study) budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.

    - Estimator guarantee (Brennan Ch.12 balanced design)
    - LOMO sensitivity
11. **W11 G-theory 桥接**: Section 3.1 增加 "G-theory for ML Practitioners" 段落，用 ML 术语解释 facet/universe/D-study 概念
12. **W14 Henderson I justification**: Method section 增加段落解释为什么选 Henderson I 而非 tetrachoric — "For balanced fully-crossed designs, Henderson Method I produces unbiased variance component estimates regardless of the distributional form of the observations (Brennan 2001, Ch. 12). Tetrachoric correlation addresses a different problem (estimating latent continuous association from observed binary data) and is not directly applicable to variance component estimation in multi-facet designs."
13. **W15 Item resampling**: 明确 1000 bootstrap resamples already cover the item sampling distribution concern, replacing the 3-sample ad hoc approach
14. **W16 贡献重组**: 按 paper_framing.md 重写 Contributions list:
    - Core 1: Format-associated item-related variance concentration (item+m×i DIF)
    - Core 2: Design-dependent D-study reproducibility budgets (with bootstrap CI)
    - Auxiliary 1: Precision separability (demoted, confirmatory negative)
    - Auxiliary 2: Practitioner toolkit (D-study calculator)
15. **FR2-HELLA**: 确认 §4.3 HellaSwag n(G≥0.80) = 118（非旧值 171）。GSM8K (128) 应为最高。

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	On multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 34--38\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$3\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, the structure shifts: model$\times$prompt interaction alone accounts for approximately 9\%, while item selection drops to 17\%. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 2.3 million evaluation records across five benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.
5	
6	Existing studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluatio
…[truncated 4488 chars]

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}
5	Factorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\%, with prompt$\times$judge interaction at 12.4\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across five benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.
6	
7	\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}
8	A parallel line of work has examined individual variance sources in isolation. On the infrastructure side, \citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\% accuracy variation from numerical precision and GPU type but provided no factorial attribution. \citet{messina2026background} formalized the concept of background temperature from floating-point non-associativity in GPU computation, and \citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} proposed batch-invariant kernels to mitigate C
…[truncated 3498 chars]

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	This study establishes that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-conditional. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH), item, model, and their interactions each contribute 5--24\% with no single dominant source. Model$\times$item interaction (up to 36\%) is the hidden second-largest source: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our cross-benchmark D-study framework extends the single-benchmark G-theory approach of \citet{mesting2026hidden} to format-conditional comparison and actionable design budgets.
5	
6	The format-conditional structure implies distinct optimization strategies. For multiple-choice benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; varying prompts or seeds contributes negligible variance. For free-form benchmarks, model identity and model$\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection, and evaluation protocols should systematically vary both. The multi-facet efficiency gain is itself format-conditional: MC benchmarks show a 10--12$\times$ ratio while FF benchmarks show 21--22$\times$, indicating that single-condition evaluation of reasoning tasks incurs double the reliability penalty. BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring on single-GPU inference, which eliminates precision as a required control variable. The released D-study calculator enables practitioners to determine the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.
7	
8	Two extensions are planned. Multi-GPU tensor parallelism will test whether precision separability holds under distributed numerical non-associativity. Fine-tuning variance analysis will quantify how adaptation reshapes the item difficulty profiles that drive model$\times$item interaction.
